B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

TAK-003登革热疫苗在巴西圣保罗州2024–2025暴发期间保护青少年 - Bioengineer.org

TAK-003 dengue vaccine protects adolescents during São Paulo’s 2024–2025 outbreak - Bioengineer.org
新闻简报一项发表在eClinicalMedicine上的研究评估了TAK-003登革热疫苗在巴西圣保罗州2024–2025暴发期间的有效性。该研究由巴西马托格罗索州联邦大学、美国佛罗里达大学和耶鲁公共卫生学院的研究团队开展,分析了超过166,000例青少年(10至15岁)登革热检测数据。研究结果显示,单剂量疫苗在接种后一年内可预防约60%的有症状登革热病例和75%的住院情况,而两剂接种方案提供了更强的保护效果。研究还指出,在暴发期间,主导的病毒血清型发生了变化,DENV-3重新出现,DENV-4则在2025年初被检测到。尽管疫苗表现出良好的效果,研究人员仍强调需要长期监测,以应对潜在的风险和局限性,例如缺乏基线血清状态和轻度感染可能被低估的报告问题。研究结果表明,疫苗的保护作用不会随时间减弱,为登革热控制策略提供了积极的前景。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究是在2024–2025年登革热暴发期间在圣保罗进行的,研究结果的发表日期为2026-09-04。分析涵盖了为期一年的青少年数据。
地点与对象
该研究在巴西圣保罗州进行,重点关注10至15岁的青少年群体。研究人群包括384,546人,其中166,390人参与了分析。
事件
一项真实世界评估在TAK-003登革热疫苗上进行,结果显示单剂量仍可预防约60%的有症状登革热病例和75%的住院情况,接种后一年。该研究由巴西马托格罗索州联邦大学、美国佛罗里达大学和耶鲁公共卫生学院的研究人员报告。
规模、影响与风险
研究发现,疫苗对有症状登革热的有效性为单剂量59.2%、双剂量73.1%。对住院的有效性为单剂量74.6%、双剂量87.9%。研究还指出,在暴发期间,主导的病毒血清型发生了变化,DENV-3重新出现,DENV-4则在2025年初被检测到。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究使用了巴西强制登革热监测系统(SINAN-Dengue)和圣保罗州的疫苗接种登记数据。研究结果发表在eClinicalMedicine上,研究人员承认存在局限性,包括缺乏基线血清状态和轻度感染可能被低估的报告问题。该研究的结果被认为是初步的,需要进一步的长期监测。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

TAK-003 dengue vaccine protects adolescents during São Paulo’s 2024–2025 outbreak - Bioengineer.org

News Brief
A study published in eClinicalMedicine evaluated the TAK-003 dengue vaccine's effectiveness during the 2024–2025 outbreak in São Paulo State, Brazil. The research, conducted by teams from the Federal University of Mato Grosso do Sul, the University of Florida, and Yale School of Public Health, analyzed data from over 166,000 dengue tests among adolescents aged 10 to 15 years. The findings showed that a single dose of the Qdenga vaccine prevented about 60% of symptomatic dengue cases and 75% of hospitalizations one year post-vaccination, while the two-dose schedule provided even stronger protection. The study also noted that the dominant viral serotypes shifted during the outbreak, with DENV-3 re-emerging and DENV-4 detected in early 2025. Despite the vaccine's effectiveness, the researchers emphasized the need for long-term surveillance due to potential risks and limitations, such as the lack of baseline serostatus and the possibility of underreporting mild infections. The results suggest that the vaccine's protection does not wane over time, offering a promising outlook for dengue control strategies.
Original source text (English)
Brazil’s largest real-world evaluation of the TAK-003 dengue vaccine has delivered a verdict that public health officials around the world have been waiting to hear: protection does not fade. In a sweeping test-negative case-control study drawing on more than 166,000 dengue tests among adolescents in São Paulo State, researchers report that one year after vaccination, a single dose of the Qdenga vaccine still prevented roughly three-quarters of symptomatic dengue cases, while the recommended two-dose schedule kept its grip even firmer, blocking nearly nine in ten hospitalizations. The findings, published in eClinicalMedicine, arrive at a critical moment for dengue control, as the virus responsible for more than half a million hospitalizations worldwide each year continues its climate-driven expansion into new territories and populations. The study’s scale is unprecedented for this vaccine. Researchers from the Federal University of Mato Grosso do Sul, the University of Florida, Yale School of Public Health, and collaborating institutions harnessed Brazil’s compulsory dengue surveillance system (SINAN-Dengue) and São Paulo State’s vaccination registry, linking individual records for 384,546 adolescents aged 10 to 15 years. From 207,211 available NS1 antigen or RT-PCR tests, the analytic cohort of 166,390 individuals was assembled, comprising 65,365 laboratory-confirmed dengue cases and 101,025 test-negative controls. All participants had presented with acute febrile illness, defined as sudden-onset fever lasting up to seven days accompanied by symptoms such as headache, myalgia, rash, or retro-orbital pain. The test-negative design underlying this analysis has become the workhorse of real-world vaccine effectiveness research, pioneered during influenza vaccination campaigns and refined during the COVID-19 pandemic. Because both cases and controls are drawn from the same pool of symptomatic individuals who sought care and were tested, the approach inherently adjusts for differences in healthcare-seeking behavior between vaccinated and unvaccinated populations, a form of confounding that has historically plagued observational vaccine studies. In the adjusted models, the team used mixed-effects logistic regression with random intercepts for each of the state’s 28 surveillance areas, controlling for age, sex, self-reported race or skin color, chronic comorbidities, residence in a priority municipality, and calendar time modeled with a natural spline with five degrees of freedom. What makes this study particularly persuasive, however, is its internal fraud-detection mechanism. The researchers designated the first seven days after vaccination as a “bias indicator period,” because the live-attenuated vaccine cannot plausibly confer protection in that window. True to expectation, vaccine effectiveness during those first seven days was statistically indistinguishable from zero, at minus 2.4 percent. Then, precisely as the biology of the adaptive immune response would predict, protection surged: by days 8 to 13, effectiveness against symptomatic dengue reached 64.2 percent, climbing to 68.8 percent by days 14 to 27. That clean temporal signature argues strongly against residual confounding and lends the estimates an almost experimental quality. The headline numbers are striking on their own. Against symptomatic, virologically confirmed dengue, vaccine effectiveness was 59.2 percent (95 percent confidence interval 56.1 to 62.0) for one dose and 73.1 percent (69.1 to 76.6) for two doses. Against hospitalization, the figures were 74.6 percent (62.7 to 82.7) for one dose and 87.9 percent (73.0 to 94.6) for the second dose, the first real-world estimates ever produced for TAK-003’s second-dose protection against severe disease. The time-stratified analysis revealed something even more consequential: no waning. Point estimates for the first dose held above 60 percent at every interval and actually reached 76.8 percent at 365 or more days. The second dose maintained effectiveness between 66 and 76 percent across all periods through one year. Discover more Medical Literature & Resources The context of this performance matters enormously. Brazil endured its worst recorded dengue epidemic in 2024, with more than 6,000 deaths, followed by sustained transmission into 2025. Over the study period, the dominant viral serotypes shifted: DENV-1 and DENV-2 prevailed through 2024, while DENV-3, absent from São Paulo for over a decade, re-emerged and rose steadily, and six cases of DENV-4 were detected in early 2025. That the vaccine’s effectiveness remained stable through this serotype transition is a meaningful finding, given that the pivotal phase 3 TIDES trial had reported comparatively lower efficacy against DENV-3 (62.6 percent) than against DENV-1 (73.7 percent) or DENV-2 (97.7 percent). A caveat tempers the interpretation: serotype information was available for only 845 test-positive cases, so the analysis cannot definitively attribute the sustained protection to DENV-3 specifically. The study also overturns an earlier alarm. The team’s initial six-month evaluation, published in The Lancet Infectious Diseases, had suggested a possible decline in first-dose effectiveness after 90 days, with the estimate dropping to 49.7 percent. With extended follow-up and vastly more statistical power, that apparent waning has dissolved, likely an artifact of confounding by vaccination timing during the epidemic’s peak. The overall effectiveness estimates also rose compared with the earlier analysis, from 50.2 to 59.2 percent for the first dose, a shift the authors attribute partly to a smaller share of cases now falling within the early, unprotected post-vaccination window. Excluding the first seven days entirely, first-dose effectiveness climbs to 68.3 percent. Methodological rigor was reinforced by five sensitivity analyses, all of which reproduced the main findings. Varying the diagnostic window from five to seven days after symptom onset, incorporating IgM-based tests, relaxing the fever requirement, expanding to the entire state, and increasing spline flexibility all produced closely aligned estimates. For hospitalization, first-dose effectiveness ranged from 67.3 to 75.1 percent and second-dose effectiveness from 82.3 to 88.1 percent across all variants. As in the main analysis, secondary outcomes drew on community and hospitalized syndromic controls, chosen because they best represent vaccine uptake in the source population while minimizing testing bias. The study’s limitations are candidly acknowledged. Baseline dengue serostatus was unavailable, preventing stratification by prior infection history, though the authors note there is no plausible mechanism by which serostatus would differ systematically between vaccinated and unvaccinated adolescents in a campaign that immunized all eligible 10-to-14-year-olds regardless of exposure history. Passive surveillance may miss mild infections, serotype-specific effectiveness could not be computed, and twelve months is a short horizon in the shadow of the CYD-TDV (Dengvaxia) experience, where elevated severe-dengue risk emerged among seronegative vaccinees only beyond three years of follow-up. That earlier vaccine’s troubled history, confirmed in post-licensure evaluations in the Philippines and in Paraná State, Brazil, makes long-term TAK-003 surveillance not merely prudent but essential. The implications ripple outward from São Paulo. The WHO recommends TAK-003 for children aged 6 to 16 in high-burden areas, and Brazil’s national campaign, launched in February 2024 amid the epidemic, delivered over 2.15 million doses across 645 municipalities by the end of 2025. The demonstration that a single dose provides durable, meaningful protection opens a provocative question about dose-sparing strategies, echoing the historical arc of HPV, yellow fever, and pneumococcal conjugate vaccines, where accumulating real-world evidence eventually informed reduced-dose policies. The authors are careful to frame this as hypothesis-generating: the two-dose schedule remains the recommended regimen and outperformed one dose on every outcome. But in a world where vaccine supply, logistics, and coverage compete for finite resources, the difference between 59 and 73 percent protection against symptomatic disease may look different against the backdrop of an 8,900-dose gap between first and second dose uptake in São Paulo alone. The authors also point to a benefit for a very different population: travelers. The rapid onset of protection, measurable within one to two weeks of the first dose, positions TAK-003 as a practical tool for visitors heading into dengue-endemic regions, where the current recommendation landscape has been complicated by serostatus-dependent risk profiles for the older Dengvaxia product. Meanwhile, the researchers call for continued post-marketing surveillance, longer follow-up to assess durability beyond one year, and serotype-specific monitoring as DENV-3 continues its resurgence across South America. For now, the message from Brazil is unambiguous: TAK-003 works in the real world, its protection holds through a full year of intense, shifting transmission, and its second dose delivers the strongest shield against the hospitalizations and deaths that make dengue such a formidable public health adversary. As dengue’s geographic footprint widens with warming climates and urbanization, the largest observational evaluation of this vaccine to date provides exactly the kind of evidence that health authorities need to plan, prioritize, and, ultimately, protect the next generation at risk. Florida dengue cases spike. How to protect yourself on Labor Day weekend - Sarasota Herald-Tribune

Florida dengue cases spike. How to protect yourself on Labor Day weekend - Sarasota Herald-Tribune

News Brief
A recent surge in dengue cases has been reported in Florida, with 73 locally acquired cases as of late August, according to the Florida Department of Health. Hillsborough County is the primary hotspot, accounting for 59 of these cases, and 14 mosquito pools tested positive for DENV-2. The increase coincides with the Labor Day weekend, prompting public health advisories to urge residents to take precautions against mosquito bites. Symptoms include fever, severe headaches, and muscle pain, with the potential for severe complications like dengue hemorrhagic fever. While there is no specific treatment, early identification and management can prevent severe outcomes. The health department recommends using EPA-registered repellents and eliminating standing water to prevent mosquito breeding. Advisories have been issued for several counties, emphasizing the need for vigilance and community efforts to mitigate the spread of the virus.
Original source text (English)
News Sports Ticket Lifestyle Real Estate Advertise Obituaries eNewspaper Legals STATE Florida dengue cases spike. How to protect yourself on Labor Day weekend C. A. Bridges USA TODAY NETWORK - Florida Updated Sept. 4, 2026, 1:53 p.m. ET Locally acquired cases of dengue fever have recently surged in Florida, with 73 cases reported as of late August. Dengue is a mosquito-borne virus that causes fever, severe headaches, and muscle pain. Hillsborough County is the primary hotspot, accounting for 59 of the state's reported cases. Locally acquired cases of dengue fever, a mosquito-borne disease related to the viruses that cause West Nile and yellow fever, have surged in Florida in recent weeks. As of Aug. 29, 73 cases have been reported in the state, up from 41 the week before, according to the latest arbovirus surveillance report from the Florida Department of Health. And it's happening just as millions of Floridians are planning beach and boat trips, amusement park visits, and backyard cookouts for the long Labor Day weekend. “As families across Florida prepare to enjoy Labor Day weekend, I encourage everyone to take few simple precautions to protect themselves and their families from mosquito bites,” State Surgeon General Joseph Ladapo said in an email alert. “Use insect repellent, cover up when possible, and remove standing water around your home. Individual prevention helps protect you and your community” Buy mosquito repellent at Amazon The hotspot is Hillsborough County, where 59 of the 73 cases were reported. The health department said that 14 mosquito pools located there tested positive for DENV-2, four of them in the last week. DENV-2 is a distinct case of dengue that spreads to humans. Initial symptoms were first reported in Hillsborough in July and August, according to the agency's latest county-level summary. The rest of the cases were in Miami-Dade (7), Palm Beach (1), Pasco (1), and Pinellas (5) counties. Dengue infection happens when you get bitten by one of two species of mosquito, Aedes aegypti and Aedes albopictus. Both are found in Florida. Florida has also reported 136 cases from people who traveled to dengue-endemic countries in the two weeks before symptoms arose. Mosquito-borne illness advisories, which advise the public to be careful, have been issued for Alachua, Duval, Leon, Orange, Palm Beach, Pasco, and Walton counties. Mosquito-borne illness alerts, which means there is heightened disease activity warranting extra precautions, have been issued for Hillsborough, Miami-Dade, and Pinellas counties. In 2025, 62 cases were reported in four counties: Brevard, Hillsborough, Miami-Dade, and Pasco. Here's what to know before you head outside this Labor Day weekend. What is dengue? What are the symptoms? Dengue infections are spread by mosquitoes. Mosquitoes become infected with dengue after biting a person with the virus. Those infected mosquitoes then spread the virus to other people. "Dengue fever can be a painful, debilitating disease but is rarely fatal," the Florida Department of Health said. Symptoms appear three to 14 days after the bite of an infected mosquito and include: Sudden onset of fever Severe headache Eye pain Muscle and joint pain (giving the disease the nickname “breakbone fever”) Bleeding Gastrointestinal symptoms like vomiting and diarrhea may also be present in some cases. Dengue fever symptoms usually last four to seven days. In rare cases, dengue hemorrhagic fever can develop from dengue infection and can be fatal if not recognized and treated with supportive care. There is no treatment for dengue fever, but quick identification and management of symptoms can prevent it from becoming more severe. Where has dengue been reported in Florida? Is it spreading According to the Florida Department of Health, as of Aug. 29, there have been 73 cases of locally acquired cases of dengue reported: 59 in Hillsborough County 7 in Miami-Dade County 1 in Palm Beach County 5 in Pinellas County How to protect against dengue There is no dengue vaccine. Since dengue is spread through infected mosquito bites, you should avoid getting bit. Stay indoors in places with air conditioning and with window and/or door screens. If you must be outside when mosquitoes are active, cover up and use insect repellent on exposed skin and clothing. Use mosquito netting to protect children younger than 2 months old. The FDOH recommends using EPA-registered repellents with active ingredients including DEET, Picaridin, IR3535, Oil of lemon eucalyptus, Para-menthane-diol, or 2-undecanone, a plant-derived ingredient. It's also important to prevent mosquitos from breeding in the first place: Drain water from garbage cans, old tires, pool covers, or any other containers where water collects. Empty and clean birdbaths and pet water bowls at least once or twice a week. Protect boats and vehicles from rain with tarps that don’t accumulate water. Maintain swimming pools in good condition and ensure they are appropriately chlorinated. Empty plastic swimming pools when not in use. Stay aware of local advisories, and if you're traveling, check for any country-specific travel recommendations, health notices, and warnings. "Almost half of the world's population, about 4 billion people, live in areas with a risk of dengue," the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention said. Once returning from an area with a risk of dengue, even if you don't feel sick, the CDC recommends taking steps to avoid mosquito bites for three weeks to avoid the chance of spreading it to other people. WHO has zeroed in on 6 candidates for a dengue drug, but only 1 in late-stage trials - ThePrint

WHO has zeroed in on 6 candidates for a dengue drug, but only 1 in late-stage trials - ThePrint
新闻简报The World Health Organization (WHO) has identified six dengue drug candidates with evidence supporting further development, though only one, VIS513, has reached Phase 3 trials. The analysis, published in The Lancet Microbe, highlights the lack of safe and effective dengue-specific treatments. The WHO reported over 5 million cases and more than 5,000 deaths in 2023, with nearly half the global population at risk in tropical and subtropical regions. The Phase 3 trial for VIS513 is enrolling patients in India, Brazil, Malaysia, and Thailand, with a larger trial expected to begin in early 2027. However, the development of mosnodenvir (JNJ-1802) has stalled, with its Phase 2 study discontinued in 2024. The WHO attributes the rise in dengue cases to factors like climate change, urbanization, and population movement, and has classified the global risk as high. Current treatment remains supportive, focusing on managing symptoms and monitoring for severe disease.
查看新闻五要素
时间
The report was published on September 8, 2026. The WHO analysis was published this month, and the Phase 3 trial for VIS513 is scheduled to begin in the first quarter of 2027.
地点与对象
The WHO analysis is global, but the Phase 3 trial for VIS513 is specifically mentioned to be enrolling patients in India, Brazil, Malaysia, and Thailand. Dengue is endemic in more than 100 countries, with nearly half the world’s population in tropical and subtropical regions at risk.
事件
The World Health Organization (WHO) has identified six dengue drug candidates with evidence warranting further development, with only VIS513 reaching Phase 3 trials. The analysis was published in The Lancet Microbe, and the WHO highlighted the absence of safe and effective dengue-specific treatments as a gap in the product landscape for care.
规模、影响与风险
The WHO reported over 5 million cases and more than 5,000 deaths in 2023 across 80+ countries. About 80 percent of these cases were in the Americas. The global risk was assessed as high due to factors like climate change, urbanisation, and population movement. India reported 2,89,235 cases and 485 deaths in 2023.
应对、状态与不确定性
The WHO analysis is ongoing, with researchers reviewing 21 trial records. The Phase 3 trial for VIS513 is already enrolling patients in India, and a larger trial is expected to begin in 2027. The development of mosnodenvir (JNJ-1802) has stalled, and its Phase 2 field study was discontinued in 2024.

WHO has zeroed in on 6 candidates for a dengue drug, but only 1 in late-stage trials - ThePrint

News Brief
The World Health Organization (WHO) has identified six dengue drug candidates with evidence supporting further development, though only one, VIS513, has reached Phase 3 trials. The analysis, published in The Lancet Microbe, highlights the lack of safe and effective dengue-specific treatments. The WHO reported over 5 million cases and more than 5,000 deaths in 2023, with nearly half the global population at risk in tropical and subtropical regions. The Phase 3 trial for VIS513 is enrolling patients in India, Brazil, Malaysia, and Thailand, with a larger trial expected to begin in early 2027. However, the development of mosnodenvir (JNJ-1802) has stalled, with its Phase 2 study discontinued in 2024. The WHO attributes the rise in dengue cases to factors like climate change, urbanization, and population movement, and has classified the global risk as high. Current treatment remains supportive, focusing on managing symptoms and monitoring for severe disease.

Health WHO has zeroed in on 6 candidates for a dengue drug, but only 1 in late-stage trials A WHO analysis of 21 recent dengue trial records found just 6 candidates with evidence warranting further development, with only VIS513 of Serum Institute of India having reached Phase 3. Sneha Richhariya 08 September, 2026 08:30 am IST Facebook Twitter WhatsApp Linkedin So far, dengue treatment remains supportive, with doctors managing fluids, symptoms & complications and monitoring patients for signs of severe disease. | ANI Follow Us : Text Size: A- A+ New Delhi: There is still no specific medicine to treat dengue, the Aedes mosquito-borne viral infection that causes fever, headache, muscle and joint pain and, in some patients, can progress to bleeding, shock, organ failure and death. LATEST Satya Niketan building collapse: PG operator Sudhanshu arres Oxygen cylinder blast in Delhi’s Okhla leaves one dead Illegal timber sleepers seized in J-K’s Doda; six labo ‘Hybrid terrorist’ arrested as terror module bus Justice Bhati sworn in as chief justice of J-K, Ladakh high Shopkeeper assaulted in JK’s Reasi, four arrested Teacher candidates’ protest for more posts in recruitm Philippines institute ties up with Odisha to boost indigenou 22 BRS MLAs suspended from T’gana Assembly for remaind India start firm favourites in high-stakes semifinal against Iron transporting protein may help early diagnosis of heart Arunachal integral to India’s unity, Northeast key driver of Why should BCCI, state bodies be not governed by National Sp Satya Niketan building collapse: PG operator Sudhanshu arres Oxygen cylinder blast in Delhi’s Okhla leaves one dead Illegal timber sleepers seized in J-K’s Doda; six labo ‘Hybrid terrorist’ arrested as terror module bus Justice Bhati sworn in as chief justice of J-K, Ladakh high Shopkeeper assaulted in JK’s Reasi, four arrested Teacher candidates’ protest for more posts in recruitm Philippines institute ties up with Odisha to boost indigenou 22 BRS MLAs suspended from T’gana Assembly for remaind India start firm favourites in high-stakes semifinal against Iron transporting protein may help early diagnosis of heart Arunachal integral to India’s unity, Northeast key driver of Why should BCCI, state bodies be not governed by National Sp Now, a World Health Organization (WHO) analysis of the global dengue drug pipeline, published this month in The Lancet Microbe, has identified six candidates with evidence supporting further development, although only one of the six has reached Phase 3 trials. Researchers searched the WHO International Clinical Trials Registry Platform (ICTRP), which brings together trial information from 20 registries across all six WHO regions. Show Full Article The search retrieved 182 dengue clinical-trial records registered between 2005 and 2025. Of these, 74 had been last updated in 2024 or 2025. Researchers then identified 21 of these 74 records, involving 15 medicinal products being investigated for dengue treatment, for a detailed review, including assessment of available efficacy evidence. Dengue is caused by four virus serotypes and transmitted by Aedes mosquitoes. It is endemic in more than 100 countries, with nearly half the world’s population in tropical and subtropical regions at risk. Most infections are not severe, but some patients develop severe dengue. A subsequent infection with a different serotype can carry a higher risk of severe disease. The WHO reported a sharp global rise in dengue in 2023, with more than 5 million cases and over 5,000 deaths across 80+ countries and territories. About 80 percent of reported cases were in the Americas. The WHO attributed the spread to factors including climate change, expanding Aedes mosquito ranges, urbanisation and population movement, and assessed the global risk as high. In 2023, the WHO declared dengue its highest grade of emergency after a surge in cases and deaths. That year, India reported 2,89,235 dengue cases and 485 deaths. The emergency “highlighted the absence of safe and effective dengue-specific treatments as a gap in the product landscape for care for people with dengue,” the WHO analysis said. So far, dengue treatment remains supportive, with doctors managing fluids, symptoms and complications and monitoring patients for signs of severe disease. Also read: India’s first dengue vaccine has been in the making for 17 yrs. DengiAll has the world excited The 6 candidates, and who is developing them The six candidates identified by the WHO are taking different approaches to tackling dengue. Some aim to stop the virus from multiplying, while others try to block the virus using laboratory-made antibodies or control the body’s inflammatory response. One of the most advanced antiviral candidates is mosnodenvir (JNJ-1802), which works by targeting a protein called NS4B that the dengue virus needs to make copies of itself. The drug was developed by Johnson & Johnson (J&J) in the US, with research contributions from KU Leuven in Belgium. Graphics: Shruti Naithani/ThePrint But its development has since stalled. J&J discontinued its Phase 2 field study in 2024 as part of a strategic reprioritisation of its infectious-disease programme. The company said the decision was not related to safety concerns. Another approach involves using an existing drug for a new purpose. Fenretinide (ISLA-101), an older medicine, is being repurposed by Australia’s Island Pharmaceuticals as a potential dengue treatment and preventive therapy. Laboratory studies have shown activity against all four dengue virus types, and the company is now studying whether this can translate into a useful treatment in patients. Like mosnodenvir, EYU688, being developed by Novartis in Switzerland, is designed to stop the virus from multiplying by targeting the NS4B protein. Unlike the two drugs that have already completed Phase 2 testing, EYU688 remains in Phase 2, where researchers are assessing whether it can reduce the amount of virus in the blood, shorten fever and improve other symptoms. The other two candidates use antibodies rather than conventional antiviral drugs. VIS513, originally developed by US-based biotech company Visterra and later licensed to the Serum Institute of India (SII), is a laboratory-made antibody designed to recognise and attach to the dengue virus and block its ability to infect cells. It has now become the most advanced out of the six drugs. Its Phase 3 trial in India is already enrolling adults and children aged five years and above. Separately, SII partnered with the Drugs for Neglected Diseases initiative (DNDi) in July this year for international testing. The larger Phase 3 trial, expected to enroll around 1,000 patients across Brazil, Malaysia and Thailand, is scheduled to begin in the first quarter of 2027. The fifth candidate AV-1, being developed by US-based AbViro, is designed to attach to a protein on the surface of the dengue virus and prevent it from causing further infection. It is still in Phase 2 trials. The sixth candidate, Resomelagon (AP1189), takes a different route altogether. Developed by Sweden-based SynAct Pharma, it does not directly attack the virus. Instead, it aims to regulate the body’s inflammatory response to infection. This is important because an excessive immune response can contribute to serious complications in dengue. The drug is entering Phase 2 testing in Brazil. When the WHO conducted its analysis, three candidates — mosnodenvir, fenretinide and VIS513 — had completed industry-sponsored Phase 2 trials in adults and had shown efficacy signals such as reduced amounts of virus in the blood or shorter duration of symptoms. EYU688 and AV-1 were still being tested in Phase 2, while Resomelagon was entering Phase 2. What happened to older drugs, and what next The six candidates identified by the WHO emerged after years of attempts to repurpose existing medicines for dengue. The analysis looked at 13 Phase 2 or Phase 3 trials involving nine such drugs, but the evidence was either inconclusive or did not support further development. Montelukast, commonly used for asthma and allergies, showed promise in a small trial but failed to reduce severe dengue in a larger study. Rupatadine, an anti-allergy drug, did not reduce progression to severe disease. Eltrombopag, used to treat low platelet counts, increased platelet numbers in dengue patients but did not reduce bleeding. Other drugs, including celgosivir (an antiviral), chloroquine (an antimalarial), lovastatin (a cholesterol-lowering drug) and prednisolone (a steroid), also failed to show convincing benefits. Ribavirin, an antiviral used for other infections, fared no better in early testing. It showed no protective effect against dengue in an animal study, and has no published human trial data to support its use. These results highlight a key challenge in dengue drug development. Reducing the amount of virus in the blood does not necessarily translate into faster recovery or a lower risk of severe disease. WHO therefore said future trials need to measure outcomes that directly benefit patients. For non-severe dengue, WHO said that there is need for a safe treatment that works against all four dengue serotypes, shortens illness and, ideally, prevents progression to severe disease. It should preferably be an oral, short-course treatment that is easy to administer, requires minimal specialised storage and is affordable. For severe dengue, the WHO said, the priority is to prevent progression to organ failure requiring intensive medical support. The WHO also recommends combining drugs where possible to reduce the risk of resistance, along with rapid diagnostic tests to confirm dengue infection and enable treatment at an early stage. (Edited by Ajeet Tiwari) Also read: India gets first dengue vaccine. 菲律宾卫生部8月2日至15日记录超过15000例登革热病例,高于前一时期 - GMA Network

DOH logs over 15k dengue cases from Aug 2 to 15, higher than previous period - GMA Network
新闻简报菲律宾卫生部(DOH)报告登革热病例激增,8月2日至15日记录了15,763例病例,比前一个两周时期(2026年7月19日至8月1日)增加了9%。这使得2026年登革热病例总数达到128,634例,比2025年同期的209,631例下降了39%。登革热是一种由蚊子传播的病毒感染,在热带地区如菲律宾较为常见。卫生部强调,对于高烧和身体疼痛等症状,应寻求医疗帮助,并呼吁公众避免涉水、保持卫生并清除蚊子繁殖地。卫生部还表示已准备好应对潜在的病例激增,通过为血库和实验室检测储备物资。世界卫生组织(WHO)指出,登革热可能引发严重并发症,特别是对于感染第二种血清型的人群。
查看新闻五要素
时间
菲律宾卫生部于2026年8月29日报告了这些数据。所涵盖的时间段为2026年8月2日至15日。
地点与对象
该报告涉及菲律宾全国范围内的登革热病例。受影响的人群包括在指定时间段内感染登革热的个体。
事件
菲律宾卫生部记录了2026年8月2日至15日的15,763例登革热病例,比前一个两周时期(2026年7月19日至8月1日)增加了9%。
规模、影响与风险
截至2026年,菲律宾卫生部已记录128,634例登革热病例,当前数量比2025年同期的209,631例下降了39%。登革热是一种由蚊子传播的病毒感染,感染第二种血清型的人群风险更高。
应对、状态与不确定性
菲律宾卫生部已准备好应对登革热病例的潜在激增,为血库和实验室检测储备了物资。他们还呼吁公众避免涉水、保持卫生并清除蚊子繁殖地。

DOH logs over 15k dengue cases from Aug 2 to 15, higher than previous period - GMA Network

News Brief
The Department of Health (DOH) in the Philippines reported a surge in dengue cases, logging 15,763 cases from August 2 to 15, 2026, which is nine percent higher than the previous two-week period (July 19 to August 1, 2026). This brings the total number of dengue cases in 2026 to 128,634, a 39% decrease compared to the same period in 2025. Dengue, a viral infection transmitted by mosquitoes, is common in tropical regions like the Philippines. The DOH emphasized the importance of seeking medical attention for symptoms such as high fever and body aches, and urged the public to avoid floodwater, maintain hygiene, and eliminate mosquito breeding sites. The DOH also stated it is prepared to manage potential surges by stocking supplies for blood banks and laboratory testing. Dengue can lead to severe complications, particularly for those infected with a second serotype, as noted by the World Health Organization (WHO).

This is nine percent higher from the 14,409 dengue cases recorded from July 19 to August 1. So far, DOH has logged 128,634 dengue cases in 2026. The current number of dengue cases is 39% lower than the 209,631 cases in the same period in 2025. The dengue virus has four distinct but closely related serotypes: DENV-1, DENV-2, DENV-3 and DENV-4. A person can be infected with more than one serotype over their lifetime. According to the World Health Organization (WHO), people who are infected a second time with a different dengue serotype have a higher risk of developing severe dengue. There is no specific treatment for dengue. 佛罗里达州正面临又一波病毒威胁激增:登革热

Florida Is Confronting Yet Another Surging Viral Threat: Dengue Fever
新闻简报佛罗里达州正面临登革热病例激增的情况,坦帕湾时报报道称,一位名叫盖伊·斯莫尔的80岁女性可能是近一个世纪以来首例被确认的登革热本地死亡病例。佛罗里达州卫生部尚未确认诊断及死亡病例,但情况令人担忧,因为该州在2026年成为美国登革热感染病例最多的州。据CDC估计,佛罗里达州已有超过200例病例,其中至少60例被认为涉及本地传播,表明Aedes蚊子正在成功地在该州传播病毒。对此,当地官员正在使用直升机喷洒超低容量杀虫剂,以试图中断蚊子生命周期并阻止疾病传播的传播。居民被建议在喷洒期间留在室内,并清除其财产上的积水以防止蚊子繁殖。此外,该州的政治倾向和公众健康措施相关的潜在信息误导也使情况更加复杂。尽管确切的影响和长期风险尚不明确,但病例数量的上升和本地传播的出现表明,佛罗里达州的公共卫生面临日益增长的威胁。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026-09-08,事件日期为文章发布当日(2026-09-08),截至当年第三个月的第三天(2026-09-08)仍持续。
地点与对象
佛罗里达州,特别是坦帕地区,重点关注居民和当地的蚊子种群。
事件
佛罗里达州正经历登革热病例激增,佛罗里达州卫生部尚未确认诊断及死亡病例,但这种情况似乎预示着该州可能面临严峻的公共卫生挑战。该事件由坦帕湾时报和Jezebel报道,盖伊·斯莫尔这位80岁女性的死亡被怀疑为本地传播病例。
规模、影响与风险
据CDC报告,佛罗里达州在2026年已有超过200例登革热病例,其中至少60例被认为涉及本地传播。由于气候变化和全球病例增加,佛罗里达州卫生部正在采取措施中断蚊子生命周期,本地传播的风险正在上升。
应对、状态与不确定性
佛罗里达州卫生部正在进行调查并实施蚊虫控制措施,包括使用直升机喷洒杀虫剂。居民被建议在喷洒期间留在室内并清除积水。由于提及了政治和社会背景,来源评估尚不明确,且由于诊断及死亡病例尚未确认,信心程度中等。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Florida Is Confronting Yet Another Surging Viral Threat: Dengue Fever

News Brief
Florida is facing a surge in dengue fever cases, with the Tampa Bay Times reporting that an 80-year-old woman named Gay Small may have been the first confirmed local death from dengue in nearly a century. The Florida Department of Health has not yet confirmed the diagnosis and fatality, but the situation is concerning as the state leads the U.S. in dengue infections in 2026. The CDC estimates over 200 cases in Florida, with at least 60 believed to involve local transmission, indicating that Aedes mosquitoes are successfully spreading the virus within the state. In response, local authorities are using helicopters to spray ultra-low volumes of pesticides in an effort to break the mosquito life cycle and interrupt disease transmission. Residents are advised to stay indoors during spraying and remove standing water from their properties to prevent mosquito breeding. The situation is further complicated by the state's political leanings and potential misinformation surrounding public health measures. While the exact impact and long-term risk remain uncertain, the rising number of cases and local transmission suggest a growing threat to public health in Florida.
Original source text (English)
Update: The very day this post went live, came news of what might be the Tampa area's first confirmed death from dengue fever in nearly a century. According to the Tampa Bay Times , an otherwise healthy 80-year-old woman named Gay Small contracted what is believed to be dengue locally. She had not traveled or left the city limits in more than a month, according to her daughter, and usually walked at least five miles per day in the city. The Florida Department of Health has not yet confirmed the dengue diagnosis and fatality, but this seems likely to be a grim precedent for the state. Another day, another potentially deadly or debilitating virus to worry about for America's Florida snowbirds, where dengue fever is seeing another one of the concerning surges that have characterized the state in recent years. The mosquito bite-driven infection is a persistent blight on tropical locales such as Latin America and the Caribbean, but is increasingly making itself at home in the United States as well ... particularly in the Sunshine State, which is again leading the United States in dengue infections throughout 2026, according to recently updated CDC data. The specter of the disease is increasingly difficult to ignore, particularly for the residents of Tampa who sound just a little bit concerned about the helicopters now passing over their heads spraying pesticides on the city, in an effort to interrupt the mosquito life cycle before the outbreak gets worse. When you've reached the "indiscriminate spraying of poison" stage, the time for pretending a problem doesn't exist has clearly passed. The virus-causing dengue fever is suspected to have been around for going on 2,000 years at this point, and as far as we know it is entirely spread by female mosquitos of the genus Aedes . Said mosquitos were actually largely eradicated in Central and South America in the 1950s, but when those eradication programs were allowed to lapse, the disease returned with a vengeance by the 1980s and has been hyperendemic throughout the region ever since. Epidemics of dengue, meanwhile, are particularly possible because there's a very wide range of outcomes in infection: Some people show no symptoms at all, while others became severely ill. Classic symptoms include sudden fever, severe headaches, eye pain, debilitating joint pain, and gastrointestinal bleeding, with some cases progressing to hemorrhagic fever that can be fatal. If treated, only about 1% of dengue cases are expected to be fatal, but the disease can still be a nightmare to endure. And any infected person, if bitten by a mosquito, can ultimately spread the virus to many others. Most dengue cases in the U.S. are brought home by Americans who have traveled abroad, a pattern that has been in place for decades. In the last 15 years, this has typically involved annual cases in the range of 400 to 800, with only a tiny amount of transmission actually occurring in the United States. Since 2023, however, global dengue cases have surged, at least partially driven by a complex net of effects tied to climate change–warmer temperatures, for instance, decrease the time that it takes for a mosquito to become infectious after biting an infected person. And as cases in vacation destinations in Central America, South America, and the Caribbean have climbed, that has also led to a surge of dengue cases making their way back to the U.S., which in turn increases the risk of more local U.S. transmission. Cases in the U.S. shot all the way up to 3,352 in 2023 and 3,767 in 2024, before declining somewhat to 1,482 in 2025, a total that is still much higher than the recent average. This year's cases are climbing once again, however, with the CDC putting the U.S. at 1,735 cases with a third of the year still left to go. Note, those numbers exclude U.S. territories like Puerto Rico, the U.S. Virgin Islands and American Samoa, where dengue is endemic. No other state in 2026, meanwhile, has had even a fraction of the dengue fever cases of Florida, which the CDC puts above 200 at this point. Most concerningly, that involves at least 60 statewide cases that are believed to have involved local transmission, which means quite simply that Aedes mosquitos are successfully biting people in the state who are infected with dengue and then spreading the virus to new hosts. Hence, the helicopters now circling Tampa, spraying what local governments are referring to as "ultra-low volumes" of pesticides in an effort to break the mosquito spread and interrupt the chain of infection. One wonders, given the state's MAGA leanings and tendencies toward misinformation on topics like vaccines and reproductive medicine, how its residents feel about a mass-pesticide campaign. Residents are apparently being told to stay indoors during spraying, and to do their part by dumping out any containers of standing water on their property where mosquitos are able to breed. "The application method that we're supplementing here using the orange helicopter... has been proven really effective during dengue transmission with Aedes aegypti specifically," said Adriane Rogers of the Pasco County Mosquito Control District. "When using this method in conjunction with our other control tools, we will be able to stop this. This is going to take several weeks of interruption to that mosquito life cycle, to really get mosquito populations low enough that it can no longer sustain disease transmission." I know it's asking far too much to even suggest this, but such a pox descending on our Floridian doorstep really should generate a new degree of empathy for one of America's other near neighbors, Cuba, which has been utterly savaged by both dengue and the similar Chikungunya virus also carried by the same genus of mosquitos. Just 90 miles off Florida's coast, the aftereffects of hurricanes, rolling blackouts and canceled trash collection have led to overwhelming amounts of mosquito-borne disease, which have infected potentially millions of Cubans in the course of the last year while the Trump administration has turned a completely blind eye to the suffering it has helped exacerbate. Like dengue, chikungunya can cause sudden high fever and debilitating joint pain that can leave sufferers unable to stand or walk. And guess what? The CDC estimates that more than 100 U.S. travelers have also come down with chikungunya this year as well. How long before we bring that one back to the homestead for some local transmission as well? Before too much longer, the mosquito-borne menace that has crippled Cuba may well be our own problem to deal with. But hey, maybe Florida has it all under control? Considering where the state's funds designated for healthcare have actually ended up in recent years, I wouldn't count on it. President Trump will give the keynote address Wednesday evening. The prediction that this season’s El Nino phenomenon will be stronger than normal has alarmed the Ecuadorian scientific community, which has warned of its impact on the flora and fauna of the Galapagos Islands, home to the highest concentration of species not found elsewhere on Earth. The archipelago of more than 230 islands — of which only four are inhabited — is located in the middle of the Pacific Ocean, 1,000 kilometers (620 miles) off the coast of Ecuador. The climatic phenomenon occurs periodically and in the equatorial Pacific is characterized by an increase in sea temperature and intense rainfall. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Dengue demands vaccine action - daily-sun.com

News Brief
The dengue outbreak in Bangladesh is intensifying, with 1,571 new cases and nine deaths reported in the 24 hours to 8 September 2026, marking the highest single-day figures this year. As of that date, the national toll stands at 45,475 cases and 130 deaths. Health experts warn that the outbreak is expected to peak in late September or early October, driven by rainfall, warm temperatures, and climate change. The DENV-3 serotype has become dominant, potentially increasing the risk of severe illness among those previously infected with other strains. While the government has launched a three-month cleanliness and awareness campaign starting on 10 September 2026, no dengue vaccine is currently in use. Experts stress the importance of vaccination as part of a comprehensive strategy, alongside mosquito control and hospital preparedness. The World Health Organization (WHO) highlights that dengue has no specific treatment, making early detection and proper medical care critical to reducing severe-case fatality rates.
Original source text (English)
Latest Bangladesh Business World Sports Opinion Politics Diplomacy More E-Paper ☀️ 🌐 English বাংলা Russian Hindi Chinese Trending Saudi-Houthi Conflict Nepal Floods Mirza Fakhrul Tarique Rahman US-Iran Conflict × Today's Newspaper Latest World Cup Magazine 2026 Election 2026 Follow Us Print: 10 Sep 2026 Health Dengue demands vaccine action Experts warn mosquito drives alone won’t stem the rising death toll Mohammad Al Amin Published: 09 Sep 2026, 12:00 AM Updated: 09 Sep 2026, 02:58 AM Share Share options Print Copy link Facebook X WhatsApp Linkedin A A The dengue situation in the country is worsening, with 1,571 new cases and nine deaths recorded in the 24 hours to 8am on Tuesday – the highest single-day figures this year – taking the national toll to 130 deaths and 45,475 cases so far. With infections and fatalities surging and the outbreak expected to peak in late September or early October, health experts are pressing the government to adopt a national vaccine strategy alongside intensified mosquito-control measures. The Directorate General of Health Services (DGHS) data shows infections have surged sharply since June, climbing from single-digit monthly death tolls earlier in the year to 43 deaths in August alone, with 33 more recorded in just eight days of September. Case numbers followed the same trajectory: from 1,081 in January to 20,536 in August and 9,629 in the first eight days of this month. “So far I know there is no dengue vaccine in the world now,” said Prof Dr Foara Tasmim, DGHS additional director general (planning and development), confirming that while anti-mosquito measures are under way, the vaccine question has not yet reached the government’s agenda. Health experts believe the official figures understate the true scale of the outbreak, pointing to widespread underreporting and a significant number of asymptomatic cases going undetected. State Minister for Health Dr MA Muhit told journalists at the Jatiya Sangsad on Sunday that thesituation could worsen considerably, with the outbreak likely to peak in the last week of September or the first week of October. He said the projection was based on three to four years of epidemiological data showing rainfall patterns, warm temperatures and climate change driving the seasonal spike. “Unless preventive measures are intensified immediately, the number of infections and deaths could rise at an alarming rate next month,” he said. Amid the heightened risk, Prime Minister Tarique Rahman on Tuesday directed the authorities to launch a three-month special cleanliness and public awareness campaign from Saturday, involving local administrations, voluntary organisations and students. Every Saturday, volunteers and students will visit homes and establishments to identify potential Aedes breeding grounds and encourage people to keep their surroundings clean. The government has also instructed civil surgeons across the country to strengthen hospital preparedness, prioritise dengue patients for admission, increase dedicated beds where possible and provide free dengue tests at public hospitals. Virologist Prof Dr Saif Ullah Munshi, director of the Super Specialised Hospital of Bangladesh Medical University, called vaccination “a must” given the country’s repeated dengue outbreaks in recent years. “For many years, we have been emphasising the dengue vaccine, but none pay any heed to our call. Whether it is a public or private initiative, the dengue vaccine should be brought to the country in a bid to save lives,” he said. Shafiul Alam, a scientist at icddr’b, said mosquito-control drives and public awareness campaigns remain essential, but urged authorities to also explore alternatives such as Wolbachia-based control methods and vaccination. Vaccine specialist Dr Tajul Islam A Bari said three dengue vaccines currently exist worldwide, and recommended Qdenga (TAK-003), manufactured by Japan’s Takeda, as the most suitable option for Bangladesh. “Qdenga vaccine is now being used by India and it is approved by the WHO. The country should bring any dengue vaccine available in the country to control the fatality and prevent the dengue disease immediately before further worsening the situation,” he said. India’s health ministry approved its first dengue vaccine on 21 July this year for people aged four to 60. An official statement described Qdenga as “a live, attenuated tetravalent dengue vaccine developed using recombinant DNA technology,” administered in two 0.5ml doses three months apart. The World Health Organisation maintains that dengue vaccination should form part of a wider strategy encompassing vector control, case management, and community education, rather than a standalone solution. The WHO also notes there is no specific treatment for dengue, making early detection and proper medical care critical to reducing severe-case fatality rates. Adding to concerns, a study by the Institute of Epidemiology, Disease Control and Research (IEDCR) found that the DENV-3 serotype has overtaken DENV-2 as the dominant strain circulating in Bangladesh this year. Experts warn that this shift could result in more severe illness, particularly among those previously infected with a different serotype, as the dengue virus carries four distinct strains overall. The Aedes aegypti mosquito remains the primary transmission vector, with health experts noting that Bangladesh’s current intermittent rainfall is creating ideal breeding conditions for the species.
